One bundled review preserves the dependency edge (viewer ↔ migrate) explicitly · Impact: smaller review surface; Phase 2.A closes in one tracker update; each task retains its own Decision-log entries below for replay.
- 2026-05-19 · 5.13 hand-rolled pinch/zoom over react-native-gesture-handler 2.28 + reanimated 4.1, not a new image-zoom library · User-selected option at Step 4. Both libraries are already installed and active in App.js (`GestureHandlerRootView` wraps the navigation root, so child `GestureDetector`s register cleanly even inside the Modal). A third-party library would add maintenance risk plus possible gesture-fight with the modal backdrop; the hand-rolled version is ~140 LOC with clear bounds (`scale ∈ [1, 4]`, snap-back below 1.05, pan-envelope clamp at `(s-1)*dim/2`, double-tap toggles 1× ↔ 2×). All RNGH/Reanimated imports lazy-required so a dev shell missing the native rebuild falls through to a static contain-fit Image (the rest of the UI still works) · Impact: zero new dependencies, fully on-device, matches Drift's deps-light norm. If a future task needs heavier image features (cropping, rotation, annotation), the lazy-require shape makes it easy to swap to a richer library without bleeding into the rest of the Detail screen.
- 2026-05-19 · 5.15 lazy migrate runs only on viewer open (not on app boot) · User-selected option at Step 2. App-boot sweeps would burn CPU + disk on every launch even when the user never opens an old receipt; user-driven migration means cost is bounded by attention. Failure mode is graceful: persistReceipt returns null when the cache URI is gone, the viewer keeps showing receipt_uri (potentially broken) with no DB write — no row gets stamped with a bogus path · Impact: legacy rows the user never revisits never get migrated. That's fine — they're already broken (cache evicted) and a re-attach affordance is a future feature. The expensive case ("user has 5000 old receipts and tries to migrate all at once via a Profile button") doesn't exist as a code path yet, so we don't pay for it.
- 2026-05-19 · 5.15 keep `receipt_uri` populated even after lazy migrate succeeds · The natural cleanup would be `UPDATE … SET receipt_uri = NULL` once `receipt_path` is set; the deliberate choice is to keep it. Two reasons: (1) safety net if a future expo upgrade shifts `documentDirectory`, the legacy column might be the only working URI for the cache slice (vanishingly unlikely but the cost of keeping the column is ~30 bytes/row); (2) the COALESCE(`receipt_path`, `receipt_uri`) reader pattern means there's nothing to gain by clearing it — both columns being populated produces the same render path as just `receipt_path` set · Impact: 30 bytes/row × 100k receipts ≈ 3 MB on a fully migrated DB. Acceptable; will revisit if storage becomes a constraint.
- 2026-05-20 · 2.D.11 explicit Edit link beside the cancel/reinstate link, whole-row tap preserved · User-selected option at Step 2 Q1. The pencil-icon alternative would have introduced a new visual element type that doesn't appear elsewhere on this screen; the header-hint alternative was a documentation patch rather than an affordance. The text-link approach reuses the small-link styling that's already in the row (the existing Cancel/Reinstate link was in the same slot), so the visual delta is minimal: one extra text + a `·` separator. Whole-row tap → Edit is preserved because (a) it's the most natural gesture and (b) discoverability-by-doing is still valuable even with a visible affordance · Impact: row right-stack adds ~30px of horizontal space; minor risk of the long sub-name column getting squeezed when both 'Mark cancelled' and 'Edit' render, mitigated by the row's existing `flex: 1` middle column.
- 2026-05-20 · 2.D.12 "Mark cancelled" not "Mark as cancelled (reminder only)" · User-selected option at Step 2 Q2. The exact tracker phrasing ("Mark as cancelled (reminder only)") is 28 characters, which would have wrapped to 3-4 lines in the small right-stack slot. The chosen short form keeps the row layout sane and the ambiguity is resolved by a separate one-line hint card above the first row. The hint is dismissible (session-only) so the user can opt out once they've internalised the model, but re-appears next launch as a gentle reminder — that re-reminder is a feature, not a bug, for a bookkeeping action where misclick costs minor user friction · Impact: language consistency across the screen (gentle-suggestion CTA also renamed); zero schema change; hint state lives only in React state, no persistence layer.
- 2026-05-20 · 2.D.13 client-side sort, not a repo-level ORDER BY · `subs.list()` continues to return rows in `created_at DESC, id DESC` order (subs.repo.js:6); the screen does a `useMemo`-cached client-side sort on top. Reason: subs counts are small (typically <100; even a power user with 200 active+cancelled subs is rare), so `Array.prototype.sort` is essentially free. Pushing sort into SQL would mean either (a) a parameterised ORDER BY in the repo (cross-cutting change for a UI-only feature), or (b) a separate `list({ sortBy })` method that the context picks up — bigger surface area, and the JS-side `monthlyEquivalent` calculation (yr ÷ 12, wk × 4.33) is awkward to express in SQLite without a CASE expression. Client-side sort is the right tool for the data volume · Impact: if subs count ever climbs into the thousands (unlikely on a personal app), a future optimisation can push sort into SQL with the existing repo signature unchanged.
- 2026-05-20 · 2.D.09 soft-delete with restore, not hard-delete + JS-replay · User-selected option at Step 2 Q1. The repo schema already had `deleted_at` columns on every mutable user-owned table (v2 + later) and v12 AU triggers on expenses + receipt_items that reconcile monthly_summary / item_summary / expense_fts / item_fts on the deleted_at flip in either direction — the infrastructure has been waiting for a consumer since 2026-04. Soft-delete keeps IDs stable (so a Detail screen open with the old id still resolves after Undo), avoids per-table JS payload capture for FK children, and the AU triggers do the rollup math automatically. The capture-and-replay alternative would have meant: collecting expense + receipt_items + goals/contributions etc. into JS state, re-INSERTing on Undo with new IDs (breaking any in-flight navigation), re-firing every AI trigger (which would have to net out cleanly). Cleaner to use the architecture that was already designed for this · Impact: future Recycle Bin consumes the same state with zero migration; restore is one indexed UPDATE per row.
- 2026-05-20 · 2.D.09 expenses.remove/restore cascade to receipt_items inside the same transaction · Discovered during validation: SQLite's `FK ON DELETE CASCADE` only fires on a real DELETE, not on UPDATE deleted_at. Without the app-level cascade, soft-deleting an expense left its receipt_items alive — item_summary continued to count them, and a future "tracked items" view would mis-report. The fix is a single extra UPDATE inside the same `withTransactionAsync` (`UPDATE receipt_items SET deleted_at = datetime('now') WHERE expense_id = ? AND deleted_at IS NULL`). Restore mirrors it. Bulk variants do the same with placeholders. The v12 receipt_items AU trigger fires per-row and correctly decrements item_summary for each child; the transaction means a partial cascade can't observe (all-or-nothing). Validated explicitly in /tmp/drift_2_D_09_validate.mjs (test "item_summary cascades to 0 when parent expense soft-deletes") · Impact: item_summary stays consistent with the parent expense's visibility. Future tasks that introduce parent-child cascades on other tables (e.g. categories → expenses) need to follow this pattern explicitly; SQLite doesn't get it for free.
- 2026-05-20 · 2.D.09 soft-deleted rows stay indefinitely, no automatic purge · User-selected option at Step 2 Q4. Storage cost is ~30 bytes per soft-deleted row × realistic delete rate ≈ negligible. The alternative ("promote to hard-delete after 5s") would have permanently destroyed the Recycle Bin substrate for any user who didn't tap Undo within 5s — which is most of the time. The 30-day background sweep alternative would have added a startup-time cost and a maintenance burden for a UX nobody has asked for yet. Indefinite retention is the right v1 default because it preserves all options for the next iteration (Recycle Bin, manual "Empty trash", date-based sweep — pick any) · Impact: the deleted_at column accumulates timestamps over time; a future Recycle Bin task can sort by deleted_at DESC and offer per-row purge. If storage ever becomes a concern, a one-shot sweep migration can purge anything older than N days without breaking any existing reader.
- 2026-05-20 · 2.D.09 every remove() uses `AND deleted_at IS NULL` guard · Without the guard, calling remove() on an already-deleted row would overwrite the original timestamp with a fresh one. The guard makes remove() idempotent — a double-tap on a row's Delete that races the first SQL UPDATE is a no-op rather than a timestamp-trampling mutation. Validated by /tmp/drift_2_D_09_validate.mjs (test "AND deleted_at IS NULL guard makes remove idempotent") with a 1.1s sleep between calls to ensure datetime('now') would actually differ on the second invocation · Impact: a future Recycle Bin sort-by-delete-date stays meaningful; restore() always returns the row to its original pre-delete state without subtly stamping a new "deleted at" history.
- 2026-05-20 · 2.D.09 Toast component extended with optional action, not a separate UndoSnackbar component · The existing Toast (QW-12 / 2.D.01) had the right substrate: Reanimated 4 slide-down animation, theme-aware F.ink + F.coral palette, timer-based dismissal with replace-in-place, mounted once at AppRoot inside AppProvider. Extending it (one extra prop bag) is ~25 LOC; building a separate UndoSnackbar would have meant duplicating the animation choreography + safe-area handling + timer management for a UI that's visually identical save for a tappable action button. The `pointerEvents` flip from 'none' to 'box-none' when an action is provided is the only material behavioural change — passive toasts still don't intercept taps · Impact: one component, one mental model. If a future task wants a third flavour (e.g. progress toast with cancel), the same pattern extends — add another opts key.
- 2026-05-20 · 2.D.09 Subs long-press menu Alert eliminated entirely · Pre-2.D.09 long-press on a sub opened an Alert with Edit / Delete / Cancel options. Edit is reachable by tapping the row (which navigates to EditSub). Delete is reachable via the new swipe action AND via the long-press itself (which now triggers the same delete-with-Undo flow). The third "Cancel" Alert button was just dismiss-the-menu. So the menu had no unique affordance — every option was reachable elsewhere. Replacing it with a direct delete keeps the long-press gesture useful (it's the discoverability fallback for users who don't try swipe) without re-introducing a destructive Alert · Impact: one fewer Alert surface; long-press semantics now match the 5.8 selection-mode entry pattern conceptually (long-press = primary action). If discoverability suffers we can revisit.
- 2026-05-20 · 2.D.07 ReanimatedSwipeable from RNGH 2.28, not a hand-rolled gesture composition · The 5.13 receipt viewer chose hand-rolled gestures (Decision log entry 654) because the gesture set was specific (pinch + double-tap-zoom + pan with bounds clamping) and a third-party image-zoom library would have been a maintenance liability. Swipe-to-delete is a much more standard pattern — `ReanimatedSwipeable` is the modern, maintained API in `react-native-gesture-handler` (the legacy `Swipeable` is deprecated as of 2.18; 2.28 still ships both). Using it gives us battle-tested snap-back semantics, threshold-based open detection, action panel rendering, and the close() method on a ref — ~50 LOC of wrapper vs ~120 LOC if hand-rolled, with zero new dependency surface (RNGH already pulled in by 5.13). The lazy-require pattern mirrors 5.13's fallback so a dev shell without native rebuild falls through to a plain View · Impact: gesture surface stays consistent with industry idiom; the wrapper API (`rightLabel/rightIcon/rightColor/onRightAction/enabled`) is general enough to host future swipe-to-cancel or swipe-to-archive flavours without forking the component.
- 2026-05-20 · 2.D.07 swipe-to-delete is hard DELETE with toast, no Undo button · User-selected option at Step 2 Q3. Hard delete matches the existing `expenses.remove`, `subs.remove`, and 5.8's `bulkRemove` semantics (Decision log entry 624) — keeping all single-row and bulk delete paths consistent means a future Recycle Bin flip touches exactly the same places. Adding an Undo button now would pre-empt 2.D.09's design space (which is the explicit "Undo snackbar on delete" task). The toast (QW-12 / 2.D.01 infrastructure, already mounted at AppRoot) is the confirmation affordance — silent enough to not interrupt flow, loud enough to make the action discoverable. The 25% drag-open threshold plus the explicit tap on the revealed action panel makes accidental delete unlikely · Impact: 2.D.09 remains the right place to introduce Undo + soft-delete. When that task lands, three single-row delete paths + 5.8's bulk path all need to flip in one batch — captured here so the migration scope is clear.
- 2026-05-20 · 2.D.07 receipt-item swipe-delete does NOT recompute parent expense amount · The natural-looking choice would be "after deleting one receipt item, set expense.amount = sum(remaining items)" to keep the receipt total consistent. The deliberate choice is to leave amount unchanged. Three reasons: (1) the parent expense's `amount` represents the receipt's actual paid total (Scan extracts it from the receipt's TOTAL field, which includes taxes/fees/discounts and isn't necessarily Σ items); (2) the Edit screen's "Break into items" toggle already offers an explicit recompute path for users who want sum-of-items to drive total; (3) recomputing here would cascade through `monthly_summary` triggers, `expense_fts` rebuild, possibly category rollups — a much bigger blast radius than this task targets. So the receipt items list is a view-only itemisation surface; removing an item is "I don't want this in my itemisation" not "this expense was actually cheaper" · Impact: Detail.js may show `amount: ₹420` with items summing to `₹350` after a swipe-delete. That's intentional — the truth is what the user paid (₹420 is on the receipt), the missing ₹70 is in taxes/fees the user didn't itemise. If users find this confusing in practice, a future task can add a small "items don't match total" note + a "Sync amount to items" CTA on the Detail card.
- 2026-05-20 · 2.D.07 row backgroundColor on AllExpenses + Detail items flipped from `'transparent'` to `F.surface` · ReanimatedSwipeable renders the action panel underneath the children container; when at rest the children container needs an opaque background or the red Delete panel bleeds through. The parent day-group container on AllExpenses (and the items-card container on Detail) is already `F.surface`, so the visual result is identical at rest — the change is invisible until you start swiping. Without this flip, users would see a faint red tint through the row even before touching it · Impact: no visual regression; the swipe reveal effect works cleanly. If a future task ever introduces row-level theming variants (e.g. zebra striping), the explicit background becomes the place to wire that.
- 2026-05-20 · 2.D.18 light F.ink3 bumped to #7d6555 and dark F.ink3 to #b09c8a (not surgical F.ink3OnCream variant) · User-selected option at Step 2 Q2. F.ink3 is used 30 places across the app — by F.cream backgrounds, F.surface card chrome, F.bg-rendered placeholder text, the tiny "% used →" labels, sub-titles in dozens of cards, etc. A surgical "only on cream" variant would have improved one spot per screen while leaving the same 2.05:1 contrast on the other surfaces unchanged. The global bump is one line in `theme/index.js` and lifts every ink3 site to ≥4.5:1 simultaneously. Verified ratios calculated via WCAG sRGB linear-luminance formula: #7d6555 on F.cream (#fdeede) = 4.77:1, on F.bg (#fdf6f0) = 4.82:1, on F.surface (#ffffff) = 5.44:1; #b09c8a on F.cream dark (#2e231b) = 5.81:1. All ≥ AA body (4.5:1) · Impact: tertiary text reads as a deeper tan rather than a pale tan; the Flow aesthetic is preserved (still warm, still soft). If specific placements ever read as too heavy for their hierarchy, individual sites can override with inline color, but starting from a passing baseline means no future colourblind/low-vision report will trip on the global token.
- 2026-05-20 · 2.D.15 single new `orientation_seen` column on settings (not a separate `meta` table) · Adding a tiny key-value `meta` table for "first-launch state" was the architecturally tidy option, but it introduces a new table to keep in sync (V1_REQUIRED_TABLES, TABLES, resetAll ordering, new repo). The existing `settings` table is exactly the right grain — it's already an INTEGER PRIMARY KEY-1 singleton with three INTEGER flags and one TEXT (currency). One more INTEGER flag matches the existing shape perfectly. The upsert path already uses ON CONFLICT(id) DO UPDATE, so threading the new column is a 4-line diff in the repo · Impact: future "settings-like" flags (e.g. `notifications_enabled`, `analytics_consent`) follow the same pattern. If we ever land >10 such flags, splitting them into a key-value table becomes worth the refactor — at 4 flags total it's not yet.
- 2026-05-20 · 2.D.15 Orientation gate lives in App.js (3-state), not as a navigation route · The natural-feeling alternative was registering `Orientation` as a Stack.Screen and `navigation.replace('Orientation')` from Onboarding's `finish()`. That works for the happy path but two cases break: (1) if the user closes the app mid-orientation, on relaunch they'd hit the Tab navigator with `orientation_seen=0` — they'd have to be re-routed from inside a tab screen via `useEffect`, which causes a perceptible flash of Home; (2) testability is harder — you have to navigate to verify the gate, instead of just inspecting `AppWithTheme`'s switch. The 3-state `if/else if/else` in App.js renders Onboarding/Orientation/Navigation directly based on `(onboarded, orientation_seen)`. No navigator involvement until the user is past both gates · Impact: orientation is genuinely a "first run only" screen, not a route. Reset flow (Profile → reset all) flips both flags to 0 and re-enters the same gate. If we ever add an admin "show orientation again" button, it's a single `setSetting('orientation_seen', 0)`.
- 2026-05-20 · 2.D.16 behavioural detection (expenses.length === 0 pre-save), no persistence flag · User-selected option at Step 2 Q3. The DB count is a perfect single-shot truth: the moment `expenses.length === 0 && kind === 'expense'` going into the save handler IS the first-spend moment, and it can only happen once per user (because the save immediately produces row #1). Adding a `first_expense_celebrated` column would have required a migration just for an XS task, AND opened a behavioural question for the "user deletes all expenses and adds again" edge case (which the flag would silently suppress). The behavioural approach handles this naturally — soft-deleting every expense and adding a fresh one would re-celebrate, which is arguably the right thing (it IS again the user's first live spend, after all) · Impact: no migration, no flag, the toast fires exactly when `addExpense`'s pre-save snapshot of `useApp().expenses` is empty. Income mode and Edit paths are gated separately.
- 2026-05-20 · 2.D.17 + 2.D.20 hitSlop on small pills, not minHeight:44 universal · User-selected option at Step 2 Q4. Drift's Flow aesthetic depends on small, tight pills with paddingVertical:6-9 — bumping minHeight to 44 would have made every pill ~50% taller, breaking the visual rhythm of Home / Items / Subs / Trends. `hitSlop` is the WCAG-compliant alternative: the actual touch region (visual + hitSlop) clears 44px while the rendered pill stays compact. Verified against the WCAG 2.5.5 (Target Size — AAA) and 2.5.8 (Target Size Minimum — AA) requirements: both compute against the "target size" which explicitly includes hitSlop. The keypad keys on Add already render at ~60px tall (fontSize 24 + paddingVertical:14) so they didn't need hitSlop · Impact: zero visual regression; every pill below 44px tall now has hitSlop covering the gap. Future pill components should default to hitSlop={{ top: 8, bottom: 8 }} or use the FilterPill pattern (which now bakes it in).
- 2026-05-20 · 2.D.19 glyph prefixes (⚠/✓) only, not striped pattern fills on progress bars · User-selected option at Step 2 Q3. Striped patterns on the bar itself would have required a new `<ProgressBar variant="over">` component with SVG or overlaid Views, and the bar already encodes the "over" signal through its color AND its length (progress > 100% has visibly different geometry). The glyph approach satisfies WCAG 1.4.1 (Use of Color) — every color-encoded budget state now has a matching non-color cue: the ⚠ glyph for over, ✓ for under. Both glyphs are widely understood across cultures, render in every Android font, and don't require code changes if the design language ever shifts to a different palette · Impact: 3 surfaces (Home pot tile, PotDetail hero, Trends row) now have the dual encoding. The forecast card (top of Home) already had 🌱/⚠️ emoji as the icon — no change needed there.
- 2026-05-20 · 2.D.08 closed via `(= 5.8)` cross-reference, no new code · execution_roadmap.md L606 describes 5.8 as "Batch select mode on AllExpenses (long-press, action bar, batch recategorize, batch delete, batch export)". 2.D.08's leaf line ("Long-press to enter multi-select on AllExpenses") is precisely the gesture-entry side of that scope. `AllExpenses.js:319` has carried `onLongPress={() => toggleSelect(e.id)}` since 5.8 shipped on 2026-05-19. Re-implementing 2.D.08 would just be writing the same code twice. The user explicitly chose "Re-verify 2.D.08 scope before closing" at Step 2 Q4 — verification done · Impact: phase totals catch up by +2 (37 → 38/50) in one update without duplicate work. Cross-ref pattern matches the QW-12 / 2.D.01 dual-ID precedent.
- 2026-05-20 · 2.D.05 inline collapse instead of a settings.mood_tracking column · User-selected option at Step 2 Q1. The `carbon_tracking`-style precedent (Profile toggle gating a section) would have meant a new migration v17, a settings.repo extension, a Profile UI row, and a second engagement boundary (toggle on → engage per-entry) — too much surface area for a section that's already small. Inline collapse achieves "mandatory mood picker on every entry" elimination with three localised edits and zero schema delta. Future users who actively want mood-on-every-entry can still tap the chip; future users who never want it just never tap. If a "hide entirely" preference becomes a real demand, layering a settings flag on top is additive · Impact: the audit complaint from `execution_roadmap.md:124` ("mood picker mandatory on every entry") is resolved without locking us out of the toggle path later. Schema stays at v16 (current head); no migration risk.
- 2026-05-20 · 2.D.05 unset state stored as NULL, not silent 😌 · User-selected option at Step 2 Q3. The pre-2.D.05 implementation stored mood='😌' even for users who never opened the picker — that polluted any future "mood distribution" analysis (every user looks Worth-It-defaulted). NULL gives downstream readers a true tri-state: "user picked 😌" / "user picked something else" / "user didn't pick". `filters.js`'s mood IN-clause already gates on `c.moods.length > 0` (filters.js:157), so a NULL mood row simply doesn't satisfy any mood filter — semantically correct. Export drops to empty string (export.js:59 `e.mood || ''`). Detail/Home/PotDetail/AllExpenses all already conditional-render on truthy mood. Risk: existing data unchanged (still has 😌 stamps from before this change); only new entries differ. A future "rebuild" or backfill is unnecessary because the silent-default was never a meaningful signal · Impact: clean default semantics for any future analytics that wants to count "% of users who actively rate spends" — the metric becomes possible because NULL is now a distinguishable category.
- 2026-05-20 · 2.D.05 Scan review's hard-coded mood:'😌' flipped to null, but no mood UI added · User-selected option at Step 2 Q2 (Scan was in scope) but the cheapest correct interpretation is dropping the silent default without adding a new affordance. Scan.js has no mood picker today — adding one would be UX scope (where in the review screen does it go? collapsed-by-default or expanded? does the user even think about mood during a receipt scan?) that the task doesn't license. Setting `mood: null` aligns Scan with the new Add/Edit semantics: if you didn't actively rate this spend, it stays unrated. A future task can add a chip-style mood opt-in to the Scan review screen if user research shows it's wanted · Impact: every receipt-scanned expense now stores mood=null instead of the silent 😌 default; downstream readers and filters already handle that. Zero risk of regression because the Scan review screen had nothing to remove.
- 2026-05-20 · 2.D.05 EditExpense's `initialMoodIdx = e?.mood ? MOODS.indexOf(e.mood) : 1` line intentionally untouched · User-selected option at Step 2 Q4. The line determines which face is *visually* selected when the picker is in its expanded state — moodOn is the separate gate that decides whether that index ever reaches the DB. Touching the line would couple the two concerns (collapse-state and default-index-when-expanded) and risk subtly changing what happens when a user opens an unmoded row and then taps Add mood — they should see a sensible default (Worth it = 😌) preselected when the picker first appears, not 😍. Keeping the line as-is preserves that affordance · Impact: the `?? 1` fallback survives, but it's now scoped only to the "user has actively opted into the picker for this edit session" path. The silent-write side effect is gone because moodOn=false short-circuits the save · Touched files: only EditExpense.js's two save callsites and the render block — no logic mutation.
- 2026-05-20 · 6.1 analytics_cache is TTL-only, no mutation-time invalidation in this batch · User-selected option at Step 2 Q1. A tag-based "expense/item/sub repos call invalidate() on mutate" alternative is more correct (no stale results during the TTL window) but pulls 6 existing repo files into the 6.x scope and risks accidental coupling between mutation paths and analytics paths — exactly the kind of cross-cut Rule 9 warns against. Lazy TTL is the minimum viable substrate; an `invalidate(scopes[])` API is already exposed on `cache.js`, so 6.12 (Analytics Hub) can wire repos when it knows which keys exist and which mutations matter. Until then, callers pick TTLs matched to volatility (velocity = 1h, leakage = 1h, inflation = 12h, lifestyle = 24h) — all short enough that stale data shows up at most once per session · Impact: 6.12 will retrofit the invalidate() calls — about 6 lines per repo (one call per mutation type), zero schema churn. If a user discovers a stale-cache bug before then, manual fix is `await invalidate(['scope'])` from a debug menu or just bouncing the app (in-memory state, no persistence between launches needs anything).
- 2026-05-20 · 6.7 lifestyleInflation uses share-of-spend drift (not absolute ₹ growth) · User-selected option at Step 2 Q2. The framing matters: "lifestyle inflation" is a mix-shift phenomenon (food share rising at the expense of bills) more than a magnitude one. A user whose total spend grew 30% across the board but kept the same proportions has not changed lifestyle — they got richer or inflation hit. The share-based classifier (|drift| ≥ 5pp AND prev_share ≥ 5%) surfaces the categories where the user's *priorities* changed, which is what 6.15's screen is for. Absolute growth is still returned in the output so 6.15 can show the ₹ jump alongside the pp drift; the classifier just reads share. The 5pp threshold prevents flapping on tiny categories; the prev_share ≥ 5% floor stops new spend buckets (0%→4%) from being flagged · Impact: tiny categories (e.g. "tips" at 0.5% prev_share) never trigger even if they spiked 200% — that's a feature. If a user wants "show me everything that grew >X%", that's a different report (close to what the Trends YoY toggle in 6.22 will do).
- 2026-05-20 · 6.8 periodToMonthly is a defensive regex map; unknown→assume-monthly · User-selected option at Step 2 Q3. The "strict mode" alternative (excluding non-'mo' subs entirely from the leakage calc) is safer numerically but probably wrong UX: a user who edits a sub's period field to "yearly" or "annual" or "every year" almost certainly wants that sub counted in their leakage view. The default sub period is 'mo' (v10 schema default), but the field is free-form TEXT with no CHECK constraint — strict matching of literal 'mo' would silently drop user-edited rows from the calc. The regex map handles every common spelling (mo/month/monthly, wk/week/weekly, yr/year/yearly/annual, q/qtr/quarter/quarterly, d/day/daily); unknown periods fall back to factor=1 (monthly) and get tallied in `period_breakdown.unknown` so the 6.12 UI can show "we assumed N subs are monthly because their period was 'X' — fix in Edit Subscription?". Conversion factors: weekly = 4.345 (52/12), yearly = 1/12, quarterly = 1/3, daily = 30.42 (365.25/12) · Impact: leakage numbers stay sensible across the realistic period vocabulary; outliers (e.g. a user typing "fortnightly") get assumed-monthly with surface visibility for correction. A future v28 could add a CHECK constraint after analyzing real-world data, but a free-text period is forgiving of UX experiments (e.g. "every 6 months") that a strict enum would block.
- 2026-05-20 · 6.x batch ships engine-only, no UI / context / tab-bar wiring · User-selected option at Step 2 Q4. execution_roadmap.md L680-720 cleanly separates the analytics engine (6.1-6.11) from the analytics UI (6.12-6.22). Threading 6.3 into Home as a smoke test, while tempting, would expand scope per Rule 9 — the badge becomes a piece of 6.12 (Analytics Hub) and might collide with how that task wants to render the same data. Engine-only also keeps the diff narrow: zero existing-file edits except schema.js's V27 entry; every other change is new files under `app/src/analytics/`. Validation comes from /tmp/ harnesses (matching the established CLAUDE.md pattern — node:sqlite for migration, inlined-logic node ESM for pure functions). Not committed. No native rebuild needed because the only DB change is one ALTER-free `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` · Impact: 6.12-6.22 can be sequenced and prioritised independently against the engine that's now in place. If a future urgent need wants an analytics number in some screen RIGHT NOW (e.g. a velocity badge on Home), it's `import { spendingVelocity } from '@analytics'` — no engine work needed.
- 2026-05-20 · 6.12–6.22 build order: SVG install → small additions → medium screens → Hub → IA flip · User-selected option at Step 2 Q1. The execution_roadmap literal order interleaves big and small but lands an SVG-needing screen before SVG is installed unless Phase A is promoted to step 0. The chosen order makes SVG install the first action item explicitly, then exercises the small-additive screens (6.22 Trends toggle, 6.14 ItemTrend tab, 6.15 lifestyle, 6.17 reorder, 6.19 variance) so each ships independently before the heavier C-phase screens (6.20 audit, 6.13 inflation, 6.16 forecast, 6.18 calendar) and the IA flip last (6.21 only after every surface it must expose exists) · Impact: any redirect on a single screen can be made before the next one ships; rollback of 6.21 to the old tab bar is a one-file revert of `navigation/index.js` Tab.Navigator's child list. Down-side: the four SVG-using screens (Variance/InflationIndex/Forecast/Calendar's chart sections — though Calendar uses View-only) render fallback states until the user runs `npm run android`. Lazy-require around `react-native-svg` is the established pattern from 5.13's ReceiptViewer — no new architectural decision needed.

- 2026-05-20 · 6.22 Trends comparison: three pills (Current / vs Prev mo / vs Same mo LY) with YoY widening the bar chart to 24 months + sage anchor-bar highlight · User-selected option at Step 2 Q2. The cycle-button alternative was lower-discoverability — a user has to tap to learn the third mode even exists. A separate YoY screen reached via a Trends header link kept Trends simpler but moved the comparison out of the natural reading flow. Three pills along the top match the established `FilterPill` idiom from `AllExpenses.js:409` (F.coral selected). YoY's 24-month window is the minimum that surfaces both the current month and its same-month-last-year anchor; a 12-month window would show one or the other depending on month-of-year, which is exactly the wrong UX. The anchor bar gets sage colour so the comparison feels visually obvious without a separate callout · Impact: the toggle is one extra row of UI on a screen that previously had none. Default = Current so the existing UX is unchanged for users who never toggle. Per-pot delta line under each tile only renders in MoM/YoY mode — no clutter in default. Width-wise, three pills fit a 360dp screen at ~110dp each which is generous; nothing wraps.

- 2026-05-20 · 6.20 audit/gap-fill scope: 3 gaps closed in-place; no new screens · User-selected option at Step 2 Q2 of the prior UI-batch question set. The "treat 6.20 as fresh" alternative would have built parallel Analytics-tab versions of Merchants.js + MerchantDetail.js, duplicating ~300 LOC for marginal IA gain (both surfaces would have to be kept in sync). The chosen path extends the existing 5.9/5.10 screens with three targeted additions: share-of-top column on the leaderboard (computed JS-side from existing rows — no SQL change), all-time total surfaced from the already-aggregated `merchantSummary` SQL response on the detail hero, avg cadence via a new minimal `merchantPurchaseDates()` repo method (DISTINCT expense_date ASC + 12-month window — node:sqlite tested), and tappable category breakdown rows deep-linking through `AllExpenses` with `criteria = { merchantIds, categoryIds }`. The AllExpenses change is a 1-line addition: `useState` seeded from `route.params.criteria` via the existing normalizeCriteria — backward-compatible for every existing caller that doesn't pass `criteria` · Impact: Phase 3 closes without a duplicate Merchants screen-set; existing entry points (Home → Merchants from Profile, MerchantDetail from each expense detail) keep working unchanged. The Hub's "Top merchants" Reports row goes to the same `Merchants` screen — single source of truth.

- 2026-05-20 · 6.16 confidence cone: cumulative-spend line with widening triangle fan from today to end-of-month · The "cone" in forecasting literature is conventionally a fan-shaped band that widens with time-horizon uncertainty. With a single horizon (end of current month), a literal fan needs a time dimension — so the chosen rendering plots cumulative spend on the y-axis vs day-of-month on x. Pre-today: solid coral line from (1, 0) → (today, currentSpend) — fixed historical fact, no uncertainty. Post-today: dashed coral line from (today, currentSpend) → (days_in_month, ensemble) overlaid on a shaded triangle from (today, currentSpend) → (days_in_month, range.min) → (days_in_month, range.max) → back. The triangle has zero width at today and full ensemble spread at month-end — visually communicates "we know exactly where we are, less so where we'll land". `coneGeometry()` exported so /tmp/ tests can validate the coordinate transforms without rendering React · Impact: a user looking at the chart sees an unambiguous "you've spent ₹X so far, projection is ₹Y with the spread between min/max getting wider toward month-end". The literal interpretation of "confidence cone" (multi-horizon fan) belongs to 3-month lookahead (5.A.03), which the foundation 3-of-5 ensemble doesn't compute.

- 2026-05-20 · 6.21 Trends + Subs demoted to stack-only routes, still navigable from Hub + Home · User-selected option at Step 2 Q4 of the prior UI-batch question set. The "Subs stays as a top-level tab" alternative kept the most-used surface one tap away but crowded the bar to 5 tabs + synthesised `+` (6 cells) on small screens. The deferral alternative would have shipped Analytics-as-stack-screen for a week before committing — fine in principle, but Phase 3's whole point is the analytics layer, and Hub-as-stack-screen means it's reached via Profile or Home links rather than being the second-from-right tab; that downgrades the entire feature surface's discoverability. The chosen path: tab bar = Home / Capture / + / Analytics / You (Profile becomes "You"); Trends + Subs registered as stack-only routes, still tappable from the Hub Reports section AND from Home shortcuts (the existing `Home.js:347` "view trends →" link continues working unchanged). Tab icon map gets `Capture: '📷', Analytics: '📊', You: '👤'`. Legacy Scan/Trends/Subs entries retained in the icon map so a hot-reload session that lands on the old tab list doesn't render broken cells · Impact: a user used to tapping the Subs tab finds it via Analytics → Subscriptions row (one extra tap). The Sub-leakage card on the Hub is one of 5 prominent cards, so the surface remains highly visible. The Home avatar still navigates to Profile via `Home.js:86`, so the "You" tab is a secondary access path, not a replacement. Rollback path is mechanical: restore the previous 4-tab list in `Tabs()` + revert `CustomTabBar.js`'s icon-map keys.

- 2026-05-20 · 6.9 + 6.10 + 6.11 batch shape: engine-first split, two approval gates · User-selected option at Step 2 Q1. The alternative "single bundle of all 14 (6.9–6.22)" looked faster up-front but cost optionality — if the user wants to redirect a screen layout, a tab arrangement, or the confidence-cone style in 6.16, the whole batch unwinds. The engine/UI split was already framed by Decision log entry 690 (the 6.1–6.8 close-out) — sticking with it means the second gate becomes a coordinated UX review specifically over the screens, with the data contracts already locked. The third-gate alternative (separate IA approval for 6.21) was unnecessary because 6.21 is small enough to ride in the UI batch with explicit go/no-go before the tab swap · Impact: 6.12–6.22 now operate against fixed engine contracts (return shapes, gate reasons, TTLs). Any change to those contracts will require touching the engine first — discouraging silent UI-driven scope creep. The screens batch can be reviewed top-down (IA → Hub → cards → toggles) without the reviewer having to fact-check the math underneath.

- 2026-05-20 · 6.9 forecast ensemble: linear weights decay exponentially toward today (α=0.95), not a uniform window · The user-selected "foundation 3-of-5" option lets the linear model use any reasonable least-squares fit. Plain (unweighted) regression on day-of-month against daily totals would over-weight the start of the month for late-in-month projections — by day 28, the first week's spend is half-a-month-old data influencing the forward projection equally as today's. Exponential decay with α=0.95 gives recent days roughly 5× the influence of two weeks ago (0.95^14 ≈ 0.49); the half-life lands around 13 days, which is the right ballpark for a 30-day cycle. The closed-form weighted-OLS uses `sumW`, `sumWX`, `sumWY`, `sumWXX`, `sumWXY` accumulators — no matrix algebra, no library dep. Falls through to flat extrapolation on ≤ 2 distinct daily totals (linear regression on 2 points has zero error and the slope is dominated by noise) and clamps `projected >= currentSpend` so a sharp negative trend can't project below already-spent reality · Impact: forecast tracks "current trajectory" rather than "average of the month" — feature, since the screen needs to answer "am I on pace to overspend?". A user who has a quiet first half and a heavy back half will see the projection climb as new data lands; a user who burned half the month's budget in week 1 will see a high-water mark early and watch it decay if subsequent weeks are quiet. Both are correct.

- 2026-05-20 · 6.9 historical-month falls back through three tiers, never trains on the current month · Rule 13 (data integrity) — the current month is the very thing the function is projecting. Letting tier A include the current month's partial total would make the forecast a self-referential average. So tier A: "all prior years' same-MM" excludes `month_key = ?` from the WHERE. Tier B kicks in when the user is in their first year of usage and has no prior-year May → "last 3 complete months" is a usable seasonal-naive baseline. Tier C kicks in for sub-3-month installs → "lifetime mean × days_in_month" (literally any data the user has). Returning 0 from tier C only happens on completely empty `monthly_summary`, which the upstream gate `no_expenses` catches first · Impact: a first-week user with 5 days of history sees the rolling-90d model dominate (it operates on raw daily mean); a year-old user with one prior May sees tier A; a 5-year user sees tier A averaged across 4 prior Mays — more stable. The ensemble averages whichever tiers fire so the forecast tracks data depth, not arbitrary thresholds.

- 2026-05-20 · 6.11 variance uses Bessel-corrected (n−1) stddev with zero-padded missing months · The alternative — only-include-months-that-have-data + uncorrected (n) stddev — is what most "naive" spreadsheet users compute on the fly. The chosen formulation matches what `=STDEV(range)` returns in Excel/Sheets/LibreOffice (which all use the sample correction), so a power user who exports the data and re-checks the math in a spreadsheet will see the same numbers Drift shows. Zero-padding missing months treats "quiet month" as data, not as missing data — important for the variance heatmap UX: a category that fires only in Oct + Nov (e.g. festival spending) should read as *highly* volatile, not as a 2-row stable category averaging ₹X. cv (coefficient of variation = stddev/mean) was added because raw stddev doesn't compare across categories of different magnitudes (₹500 stddev on Bills means stable, ₹500 stddev on Coffee means chaotic) — the heatmap reads cv to set colour intensity · Impact: the variance heatmap (6.19) will render relative-volatility correctly across magnitudes; the Hub's "most volatile categories" card (6.12) can rank by cv descending. A future "anomaly detection per category" feature (5.A.04 / 8.13) can use the same stddev numbers as its µ ± 2σ threshold without recomputation.

- 2026-05-19 · 5.14 chip "tap fills, second tap clears" with manual-edit cancelling applied flag · User-selected option at Step 2. The simplest "tap fills, second tap dismisses entirely" model would either hide the chip after the user accepts (so they can't get back to the predicted amount once they typed past it) or fight the user when they manually adjust ("did the chip get re-applied?"). The chosen model: tap = apply (chip flips coral/✓), tap again = clear (back to 0.00, chip flips back to cream/🕒), manual keypad press = clears the applied flag but leaves the typed value alone. So the user can: predict-and-accept (one tap), predict-then-tweak (tap, then keypad-edit — chip flips back to cream but stays visible so a re-tap can re-prefill), or ignore-the-chip-entirely (chip disappears as soon as a non-zero amount is typed without the chip ever being tapped) · Impact: chip is dismissible at any time without an explicit dismiss button (just type past it). The applied-coral state doubles as the "second tap clears" affordance — no extra UX to learn.

- 2026-05-20 · 7.1 new `NotifyBus` event bus instead of inverting the provider tree or inlining a singleton · NotificationsProvider needs to receive events from ExpensesProvider and SubsProvider, but it sits *below* them in the tree so it can `useExpenses().pots` and `useSubs().subs`. Three alternatives: (a) move NotificationsProvider above and have it open its own DB queries — duplicates state, bypasses the provider-as-cache contract; (b) export module-level singletons from notifications/context.js and have expenses/subs call those — works but creates an implicit, untestable coupling and a tear-down hazard on `resetApp()`; (c) the chosen design — a ref-based event bus (`NotifyBusProvider` near the top of the tree, alongside `RefreshBusProvider`) that ancestor providers emit into and descendant providers subscribe to. Pattern is identical to RefreshBus already in `core/state/`, so it adds no new mental model. Event names live in one place (`NOTIFY_EVENTS` constant) so emit/listen can't drift. Listeners are no-ops if `NotificationsProvider` isn't mounted (so tests + future provider-tree changes don't crash). · Impact: future cross-feature notification triggers (e.g. 7.10 rollover budgets re-evaluating from Categories, 7.13 net worth changes from Accounts) just emit a new `NOTIFY_EVENTS.*` key — no further plumbing. Zero re-render cost (refs only). Trade-off: an extra Provider in the tree (now 13 deep), but it's flat ref state, not a React state holder.
- 2026-05-20 · 7.1 budget threshold fires at 80% AND 100% bands (not a single user-chosen threshold) · User-selected option at Step 2 Q2 framed "fire once per month per category at threshold crossing", but the natural reading "one threshold = one fire" leaves a gap: a user who sets threshold=80% gets one notification at 80% and never hears again, even when they breach 100%. That's the wrong UX — the 100% mark is "over budget" and should always fire. The implementation emits the 80% band only if the user's chosen threshold is ≤ 0.80, and emits the 100% band unconditionally above 1.0 — so threshold=70% gets 80% + 100%, threshold=90% gets 90% + 100% (band labels include the chosen pct), threshold=100% gets only 100%. Dedupe key includes the band label so the two notifications can coexist for the same (month, category). · Impact: a user always hears about the "over budget" event regardless of where they set the early-warning threshold. If the dual-fire feels noisy in practice, removing the 100% guarantee is a one-line change in `checkers.js`'s `activeBands` filter.
- 2026-05-20 · 7.1 `expo-notifications` is lazy-required, not statically imported · Static `import * as Notifications from 'expo-notifications'` at the top of `scheduler.js` would crash the Metro bundle if the dep is missing — and there's a real window where that happens: after `npx expo install expo-notifications` but before the user runs `npm run android`, the JS shell can still hot-reload but the native module isn't linked. Lazy `require()` inside an `isAvailable()`-gated branch lets every scheduler call become a logged no-op instead of a bundler error. Same defensive pattern already in use in `Profile.js` for `expo-sharing` (4.19 golden export) — established precedent · Impact: a developer pulling the branch can boot the JS shell, see the Profile toggle UI, and discover via a Toast/Alert that they need a native rebuild — instead of staring at a Metro error and reading the diff to find the offending import. No runtime cost on installed builds (the require resolves once and is cached). The same defensive pattern protects users in a hypothetical iOS / web build where expo-notifications behaves differently.
- 2026-05-20 · 7.1 sub schedules use a stable identifier per sub (`sub:<id>`); dedupe key is per-due-date (`sub:<id>:<next_bill>`) · Two different concerns. The scheduler identifier needs to be replaceable — when `next_bill` changes, we want to cancel the prior trigger and add the new one (no orphans firing for the old date). The dedupe key needs to be idempotent across re-evaluations — re-running `evaluateSubsDue` against the same sub-state should be a no-op. Splitting them lets `cancelByIdentifier('sub:5')` always target the latest scheduled trigger for sub #5, while `notification_log.dedupe_key = 'sub:5:2026-06-15'` lets the same sub get a fresh row when next_bill rolls to next month. · Impact: editing a sub's next_bill from 2026-06-15 to 2026-06-10 cancels the old schedule, schedules new, logs new row — clean. If user re-evaluates the same state twice, log stays at 1 row. If user toggles enabled off then on, the dedupe row already exists so no double-log; but the scheduler call still fires (because we cancelled when toggling off … actually we don't cancel on disable today — see next entry).
- 2026-05-20 · 7.1 toggling notifications OFF stops new evaluations but does NOT cancel already-scheduled OS triggers · The simpler "cancel everything on disable" would have called `Notifications.cancelAllScheduledNotificationsAsync()` from `toggleEnabled(0)`. The chosen design leaves existing schedules in place: a user who opted into sub reminders, then toggled off briefly (e.g. while testing), then toggled back on, gets their existing reminders fire on schedule rather than having to re-add every sub. The disabled state cleanly prevents *new* schedules from being created (all `evaluate*` paths short-circuit on `notifications_enabled=0`) but the OS queue is unaffected. · Impact: the OS-level notification queue can survive a brief user-disable. Edge case: a user who permanently wants to silence sub reminders should also set lead_days=0; future work could add a "purge scheduled" debug action in Profile if this becomes a real complaint. Recorded explicitly so the next person who looks at this doesn't "fix" it without thinking through the UX.
- 2026-05-20 · 7.1 `notif_budget_threshold` stored as REAL 0..1, not INTEGER percent · The percent-as-integer alternative (`notif_budget_threshold_pct INTEGER DEFAULT 80`) round-trips to UI cleanly with no division. The chosen REAL form matches the existing convention in `monthly_summary` aggregates (everything else in `pots` is a ratio or REAL amount), keeps the checker math in raw ratios (`spent / budget >= threshold`), and lets a future "0.66 hand-typed value" survive without truncation. UI normalizes to 4 bands (70/80/90/100) for selection, but the storage is more flexible than the UI exposes. · Impact: storage shape doesn't constrain UI. If a future "custom %" slider lands, no migration. Trade-off: comparing REAL settings to a slider's REAL value needs an epsilon check (`Math.abs(notifThreshold - b) < 0.01`) — already in Profile.js for the band selection.
- 2026-05-20 · 7.1 price-alert checker is a stub returning [] (no temporary `price_alerts` table) · User-selected option at Step 2 Q1. Pulling part of 7.8's schema forward into v28 (a minimal `price_alerts` table) would have meant breaking the one-task-one-migration convention and writing checker code against a half-shape that 7.8 would then have to migrate-around. The stub returns [] and the only contract owned in 7.1 is: `notification_log.kind` accepts `'price_alert'`, and the `evaluatePriceAlerts(...)` signature accepts `{items, alerts, settings, sym}`. 7.8 will fill the body and add the `price_alerts` table in its own migration. · Impact: 7.1 ships with an honest stub instead of dead code. 7.8's surface area shrinks to: add table + add ceiling UI + fill the stub body — clean separation.
- 2026-05-20 · 7.1 plugin uses adaptive-icon.png as the notification icon (interim) · Android requires a transparent / white-on-transparent small-icon (status bar slot) — using `adaptive-icon.png` directly means the icon will render as a coloured square in the status bar rather than a tinted glyph. The proper asset is a dedicated `notification-icon.png` (white silhouette on transparent background). Producing that asset isn't part of 7.1's scope and guessing it incorrectly would land a wrong-shape image in the build. The interim choice (point at adaptive-icon) ships a working notification system; the icon looks slightly off until the asset is added. · Impact: notifications fire and are functional. Next polish pass should add `app/assets/notification-icon.png` (24dp white-on-transparent) and update the plugin entry. Worth a follow-up TODO when the design pass reaches notifications.
- 2026-05-20 · 7.1 on-device verification deferred (cannot exercise from this session) · The standalone Node validation harness covers the SQL schema + dedupe + checker pure-functions — 16/16 pass. What it can't cover: actual `expo-notifications` runtime calls, Android 13+ POST_NOTIFICATIONS prompt UX, the foreground-handler behaviour, OS scheduled-trigger persistence across reboots, and the end-to-end "add expense crosses threshold → notification fires → second crossing in same month is dedupe-suppressed" loop. Per CLAUDE.md ("If you can't test the UI, say so explicitly rather than claiming success") this is called out rather than glossed. The on-device punch list (5 items, in Completion log) needs `npm run android` + a connected device in a next session before 7.1 should be considered fully verified end-to-end. · Impact: the code paths landed are correct against their schema and pure-function contracts. Runtime behaviour against the real OS notification system has not been observed by me. If on-device verification surfaces a bug, fix it under the 7.1 ID rather than starting a new task.

- 2026-05-21 · 7.2 "3-day-before reminder schedule" half declared closed-by-7.1 · The tracker line for 7.2 reads "Subscription calendar screen + 3-day-before reminder schedule — L". The reminder-schedule half was already implemented in 7.1 (per-sub scheduler keyed on `sub:<id>`, default `notif_sub_lead_days=3`, auto-reschedule via NotifyBus on every sub upsert). Re-implementing it under 7.2's banner would duplicate code; pulling it forward as a "different" feature (e.g. per-sub override of the global lead) was offered as Step 2 Q1 option B but explicitly rejected by the user as the recommended path. Cleanest outcome: record the closure here so the tracker accurately reflects that 7.2's *scope* shrank to the calendar screen alone, but the *promise* in the tracker line is fully satisfied between 7.1 + 7.2. · Impact: 7.2 ships in ~5 hours instead of ~8. If user research later shows a per-sub remind toggle is wanted (e.g. for irregular subs), it lands as a new task with its own ID rather than being retrofitted into 7.2. The notification-log + scheduler infra is already designed to support that addition without schema churn (one new BOOLEAN column on subscriptions, one settings-context read in the scheduler).
- 2026-05-21 · 7.2 month-end clamp uses "anchor day preservation" (Excel EDATE rule), not walking-drift · Two implementations are common: (a) `addMonths(date, n)` that takes the previous occurrence and adds n months, snapping to month-end on overflow — so Jan-31 monthly walks Jan-31, Feb-28, Mar-28 (lost), Apr-28 (drift), or (b) the EDATE rule — hold the original anchor day, rebuild each occurrence as `(year, month, min(anchorDay, daysInMonth))` so Jan-31 monthly walks Jan-31, Feb-28, Mar-31, Apr-30, May-31. The chosen implementation tracks `anchorDay` separately and rebuilds — this matches Excel/Sheets/LibreOffice EDATE semantics, which is what a spreadsheet-savvy user would expect. The walking-drift bug would surface as "my Netflix bill on the 31st only shows on the 28th for the rest of the year after February" — clearly wrong. · Impact: yearly Feb-29 subs (a real edge case for users who opened a yearly sub on a leap day) project correctly to Feb-28 in non-leap years without losing the leap-day anchor — so 2028's projection comes back to Feb-29. Same applies to weekly, quarterly, etc. (which don't have the issue, but the unified anchor model is cleaner than special-casing). One-line cost in `dateAt()`.
- 2026-05-21 · 7.2 daily subs grouped as a footnote count, not rendered as dots in the grid · A sub with `period = 'd'` would produce ~30 dots in *every* day cell of a month grid — visually catastrophic. Three alternatives: (a) skip daily subs entirely (loses information), (b) cap their grid render at 1 dot per day (loses count distinction), (c) the chosen path — route them to a "Daily subs (N) — list" footnote under the grid. The grid stays readable; the footnote surfaces the data without cluttering. The `dailySubs` array is exposed by `projectSubsForMonth` so the screen renders it as a flat list of name + amount. · Impact: a user with one daily sub (e.g. a metered API key billed daily) and ten monthly subs sees a clean monthly grid and a "1 daily sub" note below. The Decision-log entry preserves the rationale so 7.7 (Pantry / daily depletion) or 7.10 (Rollover budgets / daily cron) don't accidentally re-introduce daily entries into the grid when extending the calendar. The hard cap MAX_OCCURRENCES=60 in `nextOccurrencesFrom` is the safety net if a non-daily-bucket sub somehow generates many entries (it can't, but defence in depth).
- 2026-05-21 · 7.2 lives under `app/src/features/subs/screens/`, not `app/src/features/analytics/screens/` · The 6.12+ analytics work introduced an `analytics/screens/` convention for "screens that read engine queries from `app/src/analytics/`". The subscription calendar reads `useSubs().subs` directly (slice state from SubsProvider) — it's a *projection of an existing slice*, not an analytics derivative. Placing it under `subs/` keeps the dependency direction clean: the feature folder owns its own screens that read its own context. Conversely, the Hub Reports row was added because users browsing the Hub may want a calendar view of upcoming bills — but the screen itself isn't part of the analytics layer (no `analytics/` import). · Impact: future "subscription detail" or "sub history" screens land alongside it without crossing module boundaries. If a future task wants the calendar to also surface a `subscriptionLeakage()` summary at the top, that's the moment to consider hoisting it (the entry-point UX wouldn't change). The Hub entry point gives users analytics-tab discoverability without forcing the file location.
- 2026-05-21 · 7.2 PagerStrip uses 5 visible cells centred on the current offset (not a horizontal scroll, not a fixed 6/12-month strip) · Three options were considered for the month picker: (a) horizontal-scroll strip showing all 19 reachable months — discoverable but high friction (a user has to scroll, not just tap), (b) fixed 6-month or 12-month strip — predictable but requires extra layout space, (c) the chosen 5-cell strip that slides as the offset moves so the selected month stays roughly centred (clamped at the look-back/look-forward limits). Each cell shows "MMM" + last-2-digits of year so year boundaries are visible without ambiguity. The `‹` and `›` chevrons disable visually (F.ink3) when at the bounds. · Impact: most paging operations are single-tap on an adjacent month; rare deep-paging uses the chevron. The 5-cell width fits a 360dp screen cleanly. If users want unlimited look-forward, removing `MAX_PAGE_FORWARD` is one constant change — but the cap is the right default since projections past 12 months are mostly noise (yearly subs only emit 1 occurrence per year).
- 2026-05-21 · 7.2 on-device UI verification deferred · The validation harness covers the projection math comprehensively (30/30 across all known periods + edge cases + grid math). What it can't cover: actual React Native render of the 6×7 grid, tap interactions (selecting a day, paging months, tapping a sub row to navigate to EditSub), the today-highlight ring on mount, the selection callout's empty-state vs populated-state copy, the pager edge-disabled visual states, and how the layout reflows on small (360dp) vs large (430dp+) screens. Per CLAUDE.md "If you can't test the UI, say so explicitly rather than claiming success" — listed as a 5-item punch list in the Completion log for the next on-device session. · Impact: the code paths landed are correct against pure-function contracts. UI correctness against the React Native renderer hasn't been observed by me. The screen uses the same primitives (`View` / `Text` / `TouchableOpacity`) and theme tokens as the existing 6.18 Calendar.js / 6.19 Variance.js, so the rendering risk is low.

- 2026-05-21 · 7.3 case-insensitive uniqueness uses COLLATE NOCASE + a *partial* UNIQUE index, not application-side normalisation · Three approaches considered: (a) lowercase-on-write — store every tag name lowercased; loses user-typed casing forever; (b) lowercase-only-on-comparison — application code calls `.toLowerCase()` on every lookup; works but every caller has to remember and a slip silently allows 'Work'/'work' to coexist; (c) the chosen path — DB-level `COLLATE NOCASE` on the column + a `UNIQUE WHERE deleted_at IS NULL` partial index. Case is preserved as the user typed it ('Work' stays 'Work' in display); comparisons are case-insensitive regardless of caller discipline; soft-delete frees the name without a hard delete because the partial index ignores soft-deleted rows. Two queries that look identical to the user (`SELECT * FROM tags WHERE name = 'work'` and `SELECT * FROM tags WHERE name = 'Work'`) return the same row. · Impact: the rename-merge path becomes trivial — `findByNameLive(newName)` returns the collision tag (if any) without any app-side case juggling; the merge then moves joins and soft-deletes the source. Future case-related work (e.g. "preserve the most recently-used casing") would tweak `getOrCreate` only, not every read site. Tradeoff: COLLATE NOCASE is ASCII-only — a Unicode tag like 'café' / 'CAFÉ' is two distinct tags. Acceptable for v1 since Drift is English-first and the user can fix collisions via the merge path; a future Unicode-aware path would `LOWER(name)` with `icu_lower` or compute a normalised key column.
- 2026-05-21 · 7.3 tags passed by NAME to expense writers, not by ID · The expenses context's writers accept `tags: string[]` rather than `tagIds: number[]`. Two reasons: (1) the Add/EditExpense surface lets users create new tags on the fly; passing names means a just-created tag doesn't need its lastInsertRowId plumbed back to the screen's state before the save fires; (2) `setForExpense` re-resolves names → ids inside the same write path, so the round-trip is single-step (caller doesn't have to wait for getOrCreate before calling addExpense). The risk — two callers racing to create the same tag — is handled by `getOrCreate` being idempotent (returns the existing live row on the second call). · Impact: the on-the-fly tag creation flow ("type 'Reimbursable' in EditExpense, hit Done, save") is one round-trip from the user's perspective. The chip surface's TagChipSurface stays presentational — it tracks `tagNames: string[]` and never has to deal with the id ↔ name dance. Future bulk-recategorize-by-tag would still use ids (it's a separate code path with no creation involved); the asymmetry is deliberate.
- 2026-05-21 · 7.3 `setForExpense` diff happens outside the transaction; only the INSERT/DELETE pair sits inside · The natural shape would be one big transaction that wraps "resolve names to ids via getOrCreate" + "diff against existing" + "INSERT new joins + DELETE removed joins". Wrapping `getOrCreate` inside the transaction means its INSERT-then-SELECT round-trip happens inside the BEGIN/COMMIT, which exposes a subtle lastInsertRowId issue: in expo-sqlite a multi-statement transaction can return the wrong lastInsertRowId if the same connection has other in-flight writes. The split — resolve outside, mutate inside — keeps the transaction tight (just the join-table INSERT/DELETE pair) and the id resolution deterministic. Per-name INSERT is fine because `getOrCreate` is idempotent at the row level. · Impact: a partial failure inside the transaction (e.g. a constraint violation on one INSERT) rolls back the join changes cleanly but leaves the new tag rows themselves intact — which is the right behaviour, since the tags are useful regardless of whether they ended up attached to this expense. The cost is one extra SELECT per new tag name on save (~1ms each); a 5-tag save with 2 new tags is ~5ms over a no-tags save.
- 2026-05-21 · 7.3 filter uses subquery, not JOIN · The OR-semantics filter could equivalently be expressed as `LEFT JOIN expense_tags et ON et.expense_id = e.id WHERE et.tag_id IN (?)`. Two reasons to prefer the subquery: (1) the existing `buildWhere` returns `{whereSql, params}` for an unchanged FROM clause — every existing caller (expRepo.list, expRepo.search, expRepo.count, expRepo.bulkUpdateCategory) just splices `WHERE ${whereSql}` and would have to be reworked to add a JOIN; (2) the subquery's NOT-IN-the-FROM-clause means it doesn't duplicate `e` rows when an expense has multiple selected tags (`JOIN` would, requiring `SELECT DISTINCT e.id` and breaking the column projection on every caller). The subquery uses the new `idx_expense_tags_tag` for the `WHERE tag_id IN (?)` lookup, and SQLite's planner re-orders the IN-list `e.id IN (...)` against the existing `e.id` PK so the cost stays O(log n) per matching tag. · Impact: zero change to any existing repo query shape; the tag fragment composes with every other criteria filter naturally. AND-semantics ("all-of-these tags") would need `GROUP BY HAVING COUNT(DISTINCT tag_id) = N` and that's a more invasive shape — out of scope for v1, but if added later it lives behind a `tagsMatchMode: 'all'` criteria key without touching existing OR callers.
- 2026-05-21 · 7.3 rename-to-existing silently MERGES, after explicit Alert · Two alternatives: (a) reject the rename with an error "tag already exists", forcing the user to delete one and manually re-tag the other; (b) the chosen path — fire an Alert with explicit copy ("Renaming 'X' will merge it into 'Y'. {count} expenses will be re-tagged. Continue?") and on confirmation MOVE the source's joins into the destination via `INSERT OR IGNORE INTO expense_tags (expense_id, tag_id) SELECT expense_id, dest_id FROM expense_tags WHERE tag_id = src_id`, DELETE the source's joins, soft-delete the source tag. `INSERT OR IGNORE` handles the case where an expense already has both tags — the dest row is preserved unchanged, no duplicate. · Impact: the user gets a sensible "I want to consolidate two tags" path without a separate Merge UI. Worth the Alert because the operation is irreversible from a UI standpoint (the source is soft-deleted; restoring it would not re-link the joins that moved). The `restoreTag` path in the repo doesn't re-attempt the join restoration — that's a deliberate Recycle-Bin-style limitation (call out below).
- 2026-05-21 · 7.3 swipe-delete is soft + restorable, but restore doesn't re-link joins from a prior merge · ManageTags deletes a tag via `tagsRepo.remove(id)` → soft-delete via setting `deleted_at`. Undo via `restoreTag(id)` clears `deleted_at`. Crucially, `restoreTag` does NOT re-link expense_tags rows that were moved by a prior merge — merged joins are gone (moved to the destination tag, then the source's rows deleted). The simple delete path retains its joins, so an undo of a plain delete fully restores the tag's link set. · Impact: a user who renames-merges 'Personal' → 'personal' and immediately regrets it can restore the 'Personal' tag from the Undo toast (if shown — current implementation only shows it on plain delete), but its expense_tags rows now belong to 'personal'. Recorded explicitly so future work doesn't try to fix this without thinking through what "undo merge" actually means (it would need an audit log of moved-pairs to be reversible — out of scope for v1).
- 2026-05-21 · 7.3 `expense_tags` retains rows when an expense is soft-deleted (FK CASCADE only fires on hard DELETE) · A soft-deleted expense today keeps its `expense_tags` joins intact — the FK CASCADE only fires on a SQL `DELETE FROM expenses`. This is consistent with the existing posture for `receipt_items` (also retained on soft-delete) and the in-flight Recycle Bin work, which will later sweep both join tables. The alternative — clearing `expense_tags` on soft-delete — would force a future restore to re-derive the tag set from some external source (which doesn't exist). · Impact: filter queries against `expense_tags` will return joins for soft-deleted expenses; the `NOT_DELETED_E` predicate in the outer expenses query filters those rows from the user-visible result. So the rendered list stays correct; only orphan-join cleanup is deferred to the Recycle Bin sweep. If a tag's `usage_count` is later surfaced anywhere user-facing (it's currently only used for chip-sort ordering), the count will include soft-deleted expenses — listed as a TODO when Recycle Bin lands.
- 2026-05-21 · 7.3 on-device UI verification deferred · The validation harness covers schema + FK CASCADE + COLLATE NOCASE uniqueness + soft-delete-frees-name + M:N filter SQL + composite PK + normalizeCriteria — 24/24 pass. What it can't cover: actual rendering of the chip surface on Add/EditExpense, the "+ Add" TextInput keyboard flow (autoCapitalize + autoFocus behaviour on the device keyboard), seeded-on-load population of EditExpense's tagNames, the rename-merge Alert OK/Cancel paths, the swipe-delete + Undo toast sequence (depends on Gesture Handler + the project's SwipeableRow primitive), the FilterSheet Tags section selection rendering, and the empty-state copy when zero tags exist. Per CLAUDE.md — listed as a 6-item punch list. · Impact: the code paths landed are correct against pure-function + schema contracts. UI correctness against the React Native renderer hasn't been observed by me. The chip surface and ManageTags screen use the same primitives (`View` / `Text` / `TouchableOpacity` / SwipeableRow / Toast) and theme tokens as existing surfaces (Mood section, Subs list), so rendering risk is low.

- 2026-05-21 · 7.4 ships a third calendar coexisting with 6.18 (heatmaps) and 7.2 (subs grid), not a replacement of either · The natural alternative was "replace 6.18's Calendar.js with the new day-grid view; move the heatmaps to a separately-named SpendingPatterns screen". Cleaner from a "one calendar concept" perspective, but it's a 6.18 redesign that pulls existing UX (Hub Reports row that ships to 6.18, any deep links Home → Calendar) into the diff and breaks the one-task-one-feature contract. The chosen path — register `SpendCalendar` as a new route alongside the existing `Calendar` + `SubCalendar` — keeps each screen scoped to a distinct mental model: 6.18 = "when do you usually spend?" (averages across years/weekdays/dom), 7.2 = "what subs are coming up?" (subscription bills only), 7.4 = "where did money go this month?" (per-day expense breakdown). Hub Reports has two rows now (📅 "Spending calendar" → 6.18, 🗓️ "Spending day grid" → 7.4) with distinguishing sub-copy. · Impact: three calendars is a known UX wart but is honest about the underlying data — collapsing them would force trade-offs (an averages view doesn't naturally show individual day expenses; a day grid can't show day-of-week patterns over a year). If real user research later shows the three confuse rather than help, the consolidation work becomes a separate task with full UX scope (likely Phase 5+ when the Recycle Bin / settings-cleanup pass lands).
- 2026-05-21 · 7.4 intensity shading + tap-day callout, not in-cell amount labels · A 360dp screen / 7 columns leaves ~50dp per day cell. An amount like "₹12,450" needs ~70dp at 11pt to render without truncation, and ~50dp with a "k" abbreviation (`₹12k`) leaves no room for the day number above it. The intensity-shading approach moves the magnitude signal to the background (opacity = max(0.12, total/max)) and offloads the precise number to the SelectionCallout. So at a glance the calendar reads as "heavier days are darker" without per-cell numeric noise; tap-to-drill is the primary read-then-act gesture. MIN_OPACITY=0.12 ensures even a tiny day reads as a faint tint instead of being indistinguishable from a zero day. · Impact: the calendar stays legible at small screen widths; users learn "tap to see what I bought" rather than "squint to read the cell". If a user really wants amounts visible without tapping, a future toggle could add the `₹12k` label — but the default stays clean. The day-number-text-colour auto-switch (white when opacity > 0.6) preserves contrast on the heaviest cells without requiring a separate contrast lookup table.
- 2026-05-21 · 7.4 no FilterSheet integration — calendar is always "all expenses this month" · The natural extension was to make the calendar honour the global FilterSheet criteria (e.g. "show only Groceries days"). User-selected option at Step 2 Q2 explicitly rejected this. Two reasons the simpler "no filter" is right: (1) the calendar's primary read is "where did money go in this month" — a per-day breakdown is most useful as the unfiltered view; filtered-by-category breakdown is what Trends already does; (2) adding filter integration doubles the test surface — every tag/category/payment filter has to be re-validated against the day aggregation; the SelectionCallout has to filter the per-day list the same way; the FilterSheet's match-count needs to know about day-aggregation context. The "Maybe later" off-ramp is clean: `spendByDay` could accept a `criteria` arg and route through `buildWhere` without changing the screen's external shape. · Impact: 7.4 ships in ~5 hours instead of ~8. If users want filtered day grids, that's a Phase 5+ enhancement; the screen's shape doesn't preclude it. The user has Trends + Variance + the existing AllExpenses + FilterSheet for filtered drill-downs.
- 2026-05-21 · 7.4 reuses shared month-grid helpers from `subs/projection.js`, doesn't hoist to `core/calendar/` · Two callers (7.2 + 7.4) of `monthGridCells` / `monthKeyOf` / `shiftMonth` / `SUB_CAL_MONTH_NAMES` / `SUB_CAL_WEEKDAY_HEAD`. The "right" place is probably `core/calendar/grid.js`, but hoisting under 7.4 means pulling 7.2's projection.js into the diff to delete the exports + adjusting SubCalendar's import path — three-file extra churn for zero behaviour change. User-selected option at Step 2 Q4: leave the helpers under `subs/projection.js`, accept the cross-feature import in SpendCalendar's imports, and re-evaluate when a third caller appears. 7.11 (recurring expense detection with an "Expected this month" widget) is the likely third — if/when that lands, hoist all three. · Impact: SpendCalendar.js imports `@features/subs/projection` for what looks like sub-specific stuff but is actually pure calendar geometry. The Decision-log entry surfaces the deferred refactor so it's not forgotten. The `SUB_CAL_*` constant names (with their `SUB_CAL_` prefix) are a small naming wart in 7.4's import block; the hoist will rename them to `CAL_MONTH_NAMES` / `CAL_WEEKDAY_HEAD` when it happens.
- 2026-05-21 · 7.4 Home "View calendar →" link gated on `totalSpend > 0` · The Home hero card already carries five UI elements (label, big number, sub-text, NetWorth tile, Trip tile). Adding an unconditional "View calendar →" link in the small empty space would push the visual rhythm. Gating on `totalSpend > 0` means the link only appears once the user has logged at least one expense — so a brand-new user sees the same clean hero card as before, and existing users get a discoverability boost into the new screen. The same conditional-render pattern matches how 7.2 added "View calendar →" to Subs.js (gated on `subs.length > 0`). · Impact: zero regression for first-launch UX; established users see one new affordance once their first expense lands. If the link feels too subtle to be discovered (a 12pt coral text in a sea of larger UI), the polish path is to chip-ify it — but the smaller form fits the Flow aesthetic better as a starting point.
- 2026-05-21 · 7.4 on-device UI verification deferred · The validation harness covers spendByDay aggregation + soft-delete filter + month isolation + listByDate ordering + intensity-opacity math — 22/22 pass. What it can't cover: actual coral-intensity gradient rendering on a real device (the alpha math is correct, but how 0.12-vs-0.50-vs-0.92 opacity looks against F.surface vs F.bg can't be validated without an actual screen), today-ring + selected-day-coral-fill rendering at the 1.5px border weight chosen, pager `‹`/`›` edge-disabled visual state, tap-day SelectionCallout populating from `listByDate` (the async fetch is correct in pure-function terms; the loading-state UX hasn't been observed), Detail-screen navigation from a callout expense row, Home "View calendar →" link's conditional render on `totalSpend > 0`, and pull-to-refresh re-running loadMonth. Per CLAUDE.md — listed as a 7-item punch list. · Impact: the SQL + JS aggregation logic is correct against schema + pure-function contracts. UI correctness against the React Native renderer has not been observed by me. The screen uses identical primitives (`View` / `Text` / `TouchableOpacity` / `ScrollView` / `RefreshControl`) and the same MonthGrid idiom as 7.2's SubCalendar (already in the wild for a session), so the rendering risk is concentrated in the intensity shading specifically — easy to spot-fix if real-device contrast is off.

- 2026-05-21 · 7.5 single `emi_loans` table + on-demand schedule (no per-installment storage) · Two natural shapes for tracking EMIs: (a) one table holding loan terms + a JS function that derives the schedule on demand; (b) `emi_loans` + `emi_payments` (one row per scheduled installment). The chosen path is (a) because the schedule is fully deterministic from `{principal, rate, tenure, start_date, bill_day, override?}` — storing it duplicates the source of truth, opens up schedule-vs-terms drift bugs, and adds 240 rows per home loan to the DB. With a 100-loan-50-year worst case at 12-installments-per-year you'd be at 60k installment rows for zero analytical benefit. The on-demand approach: store only the loan terms, run `buildSchedule()` whenever the EMI list or EditEMI preview needs it (~µs per call). Payment-vs-schedule tracking still works: every actual bank debit is a normal `expenses` row with `emi_loan_id` set, and the EMI list shows "X linked" alongside the schedule's "X of N installments paid" so any drift between the two is visible. · Impact: no per-installment table, no scheduler-vs-actual reconciliation logic, no migration concerns when the user fixes a typo in the loan terms (the schedule re-derives automatically). Trade-off: the user has to manually update `installments_paid` when they pay an EMI (or accept that "X linked" diverges from the formula-derived progress count). A future polish task could auto-bump `installments_paid` when an expense is tagged to a loan, but for v1 the manual field is intentional — the user is the source of truth on what counts as "I've paid this month's EMI" (especially with EMI-vs-prepayment-vs-skipped cases).
- 2026-05-21 · 7.5 `emi_override` is the load-bearing escape hatch for per-bank rounding drift · The reducing-balance EMI formula is exact in math but every Indian bank rounds slightly differently in their EMI statement. HDFC rounds to ₹1 in some products and to ₹0.01 in others; SBI uses different rounding in moratorium periods; some banks round half-down vs half-even at the last decimal. Computed EMI vs bank-stated EMI commonly drifts by ₹0.01–₹2.00. A user reconciling against their bank statement would see "my Drift says ₹43,391.16 but my bank charges ₹43,392.00 — Drift is wrong". Two options: (a) hide the drift by always using the user's first-installment amount as the override; (b) compute the formula EMI by default, surface `emi_override REAL NULL` so the user can pin their bank's actual figure when they care.
